I mean, I can get frustrated or a bit worked up like anyone, especially if things are busy, but I wouldn’t call it intense mood swings. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like big shifts that feel out of proportion. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are your restlessness and impulsivity causing problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I’d say it’s not severe. At work, it’s meant a few purchase orders had to be corrected, and my planning coordinator has started giving my work an extra review, which is helpful but also a little embarrassing.\n\nAt home, I think it comes out more in conversation. Family dinners can get tense because people feel like I’m talking over them. I don’t mean to, but I can see how it lands that way. Does that count as causing problems?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get pulled off track if there’s a lot going on around me, but I’ve always thought that was pretty normal.\n\nI do try to keep myself organized, so maybe I compensate for it a bit. I guess I’d want to know what you mean by easily distracted—like distracted from work tasks, or in conversations?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not especially. It’s more like I think I’m being careful, especially with vendor spreadsheets, and then someone else catches something I missed. I could sit through a movie, probably… I mean, I don’t usually just leave in a disruptive way. But if it’s long or quiet, I do tend to find little reasons to get up—get water, check something in the kitchen, that kind of thing.

I guess I’ve always thought of it as being productive, but maybe that’s what you mean by “on the go”?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is more related to having trouble sitting still or to having trouble focusing on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the sitting still part… or maybe the settling part, if that makes sense. I can focus on something, like a planning meeting or even painting, but there’s this feeling underneath like I’m wound a little too tight and can’t quite relax into it.\n\nEven with yoga, when I’m supposed to be calm, I’ll notice I’m sort of waiting to move on to the next thing. It’s not terrible, I don’t want to overstate it, but that restless feeling is probably what made me want to talk about this. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to sit through a long, quiet activity like watching a movie without feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: I could sit through a movie, probably… I mean, I don’t usually just leave in a disruptive way. But if it’s long or quiet, I do tend to find little reasons to get up—get water, check something in the kitchen, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more noticeable in meetings that run long, or at family dinners with my in-laws when things feel tense. I’ll suddenly need to check the oven or look at some samples or just do something instead of staying in my chair. It sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but yes, I guess I do that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your restlessness and impulsivity are significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I wouldn’t say it’s causing me a lot of distress… maybe that sounds like I’m minimizing it, but I do manage. It’s more that there are little consequences.

At work, if I move too quickly, I’ve had purchase orders that needed correcting, and my planning coordinator has had to put an extra set of eyes on things. She’s very nice about it, but I do feel embarrassed.

And at home, family dinners can get tense because I guess I talk over people without meaning to. My husband has pointed it out. So… yes, it interferes some, even if I’ve tried to frame it as just being busy or energetic. Does that make sense?

sessions, I sometimes jump in with an answer before the other person has finished the question, and then I realize I missed an important qualifier.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I catch myself cutting into junior buyers’ conversations to correct a detail or add my opinion before they’ve asked for it, even though I’m trying to support them rather than take over.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: I remember in elementary school getting notes sent home because I kept popping out of my seat, talking over classmates, and rushing to answer before the teacher finished the question.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has led to corrected purchase orders and extra review having trouble focusing on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the sitting still part… or maybe the settling part, if that makes sense. I’ll suddenly need to check the oven or look at some samples or just do something instead of staying in my chair. It sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and impulsivity are significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s causing me a lot of distress… maybe that sounds like I’m minimizing it, but I do manage. It’s more that there are little consequences.\n\nAt work, if I move too quickly, I’ve had purchase orders that needed correcting, and my planning coordinator has had to put an extra set of eyes on things. She’s very nice about it, but I do feel embarrassed.\n\nAnd at home, family dinners can get tense because I guess I talk over people without meaning to. My husband has pointed it out. So… yes, it interferes some, even if I’ve tried to frame it as just being busy or energetic. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s there when I’m alone too, I think. Maybe it shows up a little differently because there isn’t anyone to notice it, so I can sort of excuse it.

But yes… what really brought me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle. Even if I’m just sitting through a planning meeting, or painting by myself, or doing yoga—things that are supposed to be calm—I still feel like I’m not quite relaxed into it.

It’s not always dramatic, but it’s kind of always humming underneath. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, thank you. Um… I guess what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling really restless lately. Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think, but sort of like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle. Even when I’m supposed to be calmly sitting through a planning meeting, or doing something relaxing like painting or yoga, I still feel like I can’t quite sit comfortably in myself. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not especially. I mean, I worry about normal things, work stuff or whether I’m keeping up with everything, but I wouldn’t say I feel anxious all the time. At least, I don’t think that’s the main issue. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Not like something looping that I can’t get rid of. I mean, I might replay a work conversation once in a while, like anyone, but it doesn’t feel intrusive or stuck in my head. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean, but I haven’t really noticed anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep patterns?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change, no. I mean, I have the occasional night where I don’t sleep perfectly, but nothing that feels really unusual or like a pattern. I’m not sure if there’s something specific I should be looking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical complaints, such as headaches or stomach issues?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I get the occasional headache like anyone, but nothing that’s been standing out to me or that I’d think to bring up. My stomach’s been okay, I think. Nothing major. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yes. I don’t think I’m reckless or anything, but I do catch myself jumping in too fast sometimes. Like in vendor negotiations, or when I’m mentoring in STEM sessions, I’ll answer before the other person has fully finished the question. Then I realize there was some qualifier I missed, and I feel a bit embarrassed. I usually try to smooth it over, but… yes, I guess that would count as acting on impulse?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I wouldn’t say I can’t focus at all. It’s more like I think I’m being careful, especially with vendor spreadsheets, and then someone else catches something I missed. Over the past couple of years our planning coordinator has found things like I transposed a SKU, or missed a freight surcharge, or carried the wrong quantity into a purchase order.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I really do try to double-check. It’s not constant, but it happens enough that I’ve noticed it. Does that fit with what you mean by trouble focusing?\n\nDoctor: Do you have intense mood swings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I can get frustrated or a bit worked up like anyone, especially if things are busy, but I wouldn’t call it intense mood swings. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like big shifts that feel out of proportion. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are your restlessness and impulsivity causing problems for you at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I’d say it’s not severe. At work, it’s meant a few purchase orders had to be corrected, and my planning coordinator has started giving my work an extra review, which is helpful but also a little embarrassing.\n\nAt home, I think it comes out more in conversation. Family dinners can get tense because people feel like I’m talking over them. I don’t mean to, but I can see how it lands that way. Does that count as causing problems?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get pulled off track if there’s a lot going on around me, but I’ve always thought that was pretty normal.\n\nI do try to keep myself organized, so maybe I compensate for it a bit. I’m trying to be better about waiting—does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself taking on more projects or tasks than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, probably… though I don’t always think of it as “taking on projects” exactly. It’s more that when I finish one thing, I tend to just move straight into the next thing.\n\nEven on weekends, I’ll do laundry, then answer a few emails, then maybe start reorganizing product samples or touch up a painting, and it just keeps going. My husband will say I never really power down. I don’t mean it in a stressed or frantic way—it just feels normal to keep moving, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, although I don’t always see it as a problem. It’s more like I just keep going from one thing to the next.\n\nEven on weekends, I’ll finish laundry and then immediately check emails, then maybe start reorganizing product samples or touch up a painting. My husband says I never really power down. Maybe… yes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s more of a problem than the restlessness. It’s quieter, I guess, so I’ve been more inclined to brush it off.

Does that count as causing problems?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get pulled off track if there’s a lot going on around me, but I’ve always thought that was pretty normal.\n\nI do try to keep myself organized, so maybe I compensate for it a bit. I guess I’d want to know what you mean by easily distracted—like distracted from work tasks, or in conversations?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting on impulse in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think it’s usually anything dramatic, but I do notice it in conversations.\n\nLike with the junior buyers, I’ll sometimes jump in to correct a detail or add my opinion before they’ve really asked for it. In my head I’m trying to be helpful or supportive, not take over, but I can see how it might come across as interrupting. I’m trying to be better about waiting—does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself taking on more projects or tasks than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, probably… though I don’t always think of it as “taking on projects” exactly. It’s more that when I finish one thing, I tend to just move straight into the next thing.\n\nEven on weekends, I’ll do laundry, then answer a few emails, then maybe start reorganizing product samples or touch up a painting, and it just keeps going. My husband will say I never really power down. I don’t mean it in a stressed or frantic way—it just feels normal to keep moving, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, although I don’t always see it as a problem. It’s more like I just keep going from one thing to the next.\n\nEven on weekends, I’ll finish laundry and then immediately check emails, then maybe start reorganizing product samples or touch up a painting. My husband says I never really power down. I guess I’ve always thought of it as being productive, but maybe that’s what you mean by “on the go”?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is more related to having trouble sitting still or to having trouble focusing on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the sitting still part… or maybe the settling part, if that makes sense. I can focus on something, like a planning meeting or even painting, but there’s this feeling underneath like I’m wound a little too tight and can’t quite relax into it.\n\nEven with yoga, when I’m supposed to be calm, I’ll notice I’m sort of waiting to move on to the next thing. It’s not terrible, I don’t want to overstate it, but that restless feeling is probably what made me want to talk about this. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to sit through a long, quiet activity like watching a movie without feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: I could sit through a movie, probably… I mean, I don’t usually just leave in a disruptive way. But if it’s long or quiet, I do tend to find little reasons to get up—get water, check something in the kitchen, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more noticeable in meetings that run long, or at family dinners with my in-laws when things feel tense. I’ll suddenly need to check the oven or look at some samples or just do something instead of staying in my chair. It sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and impulsivity are significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s causing me a lot of distress… maybe that sounds like I’m minimizing it, but I do manage. It’s more that there are little consequences.\n\nAt work, if I move too quickly, I’ve had purchase orders that needed correcting, and my planning coordinator has had to put an extra set of eyes on things. She’s very nice about it, but I do feel embarrassed.\n\nAnd at home, family dinners can get tense because I guess I talk over people without meaning to. My husband has pointed it out. So… yes, it interferes some, even if I’ve tried to frame it as just being busy or energetic. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or impulsive when you're alone, or is it mainly when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: It’s there when I’m alone too, I think. Maybe it shows up a little differently because there isn’t anyone to notice it, so I can sort of excuse it.\n\nBut yes… what really brought me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle. Even if I’m just sitting through a planning meeting, or painting by myself, or doing yoga—things that are supposed to be calm—I still feel like I’m not quite relaxed into it.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, but it’s kind of always humming underneath. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing on tasks is more of a problem for you than your restlessness or impulsive behavior?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s more of a problem than the restlessness. It’s quieter, I guess, so I’ve been more inclined to brush it off.\n\nAt work, over the past couple of years, I’ll really think I’ve checked a vendor spreadsheet carefully, and then our planning coordinator will catch something—like I transposed a SKU, or missed a freight surcharge, or carried the wrong quantity into the purchase order. Nothing catastrophic, thankfully, but enough that it’s embarrassing.\n\nSo yes, focusing is part of it. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does cause problems.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Does that count as causing problems?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get pulled off track if there’s a lot going on around me, but I’ve always thought that was pretty normal.\n\nI do try to keep myself organized, so maybe I compensate for it a bit. I guess I’d want to know what you mean by easily distracted—like distracted from work tasks, or in conversations?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting on impulse in your personal life as well, not just at work?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I don’t think it’s usually anything dramatic, but I do notice it in conversations.\n\nLike with the junior buyers, I’ll sometimes jump in to correct a detail or add my opinion before they’ve really asked for it. In my head I’m trying to be helpful or supportive, not take over, but I can see how it might come across as interrupting. I’m trying to be better about waiting—does that sound like the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself taking on more projects or tasks than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes, probably… though I don’t always think of it as “taking on projects” exactly. It’s more that when I finish one thing, I tend to just move straight into the next thing.\n\nEven on weekends, I’ll do laundry, then answer a few emails, then maybe start reorganizing product samples or touch up a painting, and it just keeps going. My husband will say I never really power down. I don’t mean it in a stressed or frantic way—it just feels normal to keep moving, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, although I don’t always see it as a problem. It’s more like I just keep going from one thing to the next.\n\nEven on weekends, I’ll finish laundry and then immediately check emails, then maybe start reorganizing product samples or touch up a painting. My husband says I never really power down. I guess I’ve always thought of it as being productive, but maybe that’s what you mean by “on the go”?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness is more related to having trouble sitting still or to having trouble focusing on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the sitting still part… or maybe the settling part, if that makes sense. I can focus on something, like a planning meeting or even painting, but there’s this feeling underneath like I’m wound a little too tight and can’t quite relax into it.\n\nEven with yoga, when I’m supposed to be calm, I’ll notice I’m sort of waiting to move on to the next thing. It’s not terrible, I don’t want to overstate it, but that restless feeling is probably what made me want to talk about this. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to sit through a long, quiet activity like watching a movie without feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: I could sit through a movie, probably… I mean, I don’t usually just leave in a disruptive way. But if it’s long or quiet, I do tend to find little reasons to get up—get water, check something in the kitchen, that kind of thing.\n\nIt’s more noticeable in meetings that run long, or at family dinners with my in-laws when things feel tense. I’ll suddenly need to check the oven or look at some samples or just do something instead of staying in my chair. It sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but yes, I guess I do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and impulsivity are significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s causing me a lot of distress… maybe that sounds like I’m minimizing it, but I do manage. It’s more that there are little consequences.\n\nAt work, if I move too quickly, I’ve had purchase orders that needed correcting, and my planning coordinator has had to put an extra set of eyes on things. She’s very nice about it, but I do feel embarrassed.\n\nAnd at home, family dinners can get tense because I guess I talk over people without meaning to. My husband has pointed it out. So… yes, it interferes some, even if I’ve tried to frame it as just being busy or energetic. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless or impulsive when you're alone, or is it mainly when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: It’s there when I’m alone too, I think. Maybe it shows up a little differently because there isn’t anyone to notice it, so I can sort of excuse it.\n\nBut yes… what really brought me in is this constant restless feeling, like I’m wound too tight and can’t fully settle. Even if I’m just sitting through a planning meeting, or painting by myself, or doing yoga—things that are supposed to be calm—I still feel like I’m not quite relaxed into it.\n\nIt’s not always dramatic, but it’s kind of always humming underneath. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focusing on tasks is more of a problem for you than your restlessness or impulsive behavior?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s more of a problem than the restlessness. It’s quieter, I guess, so I’ve been more inclined to brush it off.\n\nAt work, over the past couple of years, I’ll really think I’ve checked a vendor spreadsheet carefully, and then our planning coordinator will catch something—like I transposed a SKU, or missed a freight surcharge, or carried the wrong quantity into the purchase order. Nothing catastrophic, thankfully, but enough that it’s embarrassing.\n\nSo yes, focusing is part of it. I don’t want to overstate it, but it does cause problems.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I do finish things, especially if someone is waiting on me or there’s a clear deadline.\n\nBut… I suppose there are times when I have to reread instructions or go back and check what I was supposed to be doing because my mind has drifted. I don’t know if that counts as losing focus, or if that’s just normal? I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to paying attention to details or to controlling your impulses and activity level?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the restlessness and activity level, honestly. I mean, I do miss details sometimes, and I don’t want to dismiss that, but what really feels hard to live with is this constant wound-up feeling.\n\nEven when I’m doing something that should be calm—like sitting in a planning meeting, or painting, or even yoga—I don’t feel fully settled. It’s like I’m trying to be still on the outside, but inside I’m not quite at rest. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
